$`\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{{T}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|\nabla{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{2\left[{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(0)}}\right)-{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{\star}}\right)\right]}{{\eta}_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}{T}}+16H\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}+32H\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}`$$`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{C^{2}{\sigma}_{\mathrm{DP}}^{2}}{{\xi}^{2}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}{R}_{h}^{2}{d}_{h}+2\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}\sum_{h=1}^{H}M_{h}^{2}\left[16L^{2}\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}+\frac{1}{C_{h}^{2}}+4P_{h}^{2}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+4\frac{\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{\xi^{2}T}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\frac{R_{h}^{2}M_{h}^{2}}{C_{h}^{2}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left[\mathbb{E}_{{}_{k}}\left[\mathsf{Var}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left(\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|\right)\right]+\mathsf{Var}_{{}_{k}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|\right]\right)\right]`$

and substituting $`\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}=1/\sqrt{T}`$, $`\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}=1/(T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}\sqrt{T})`$, $`\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{DP}}}^{2}=0`$ and $`C_{h}\rightarrow\infty`$, we get,

$`\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{{T}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|\nabla{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{2\left[{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(0)}}\right)-{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{\star}}\right)\right]}{(1/\sqrt{T}){T}}+16H\frac{1}{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}\frac{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}`$$`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+32H\frac{1}{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}\frac{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}+2\frac{1}{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}\frac{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T}\sum_{h=1}^{H}M_{h}^{2}\left[16L^{2}\frac{1}{TT_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}+4P_{h}^{2}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\frac{2\left[{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(0)}}\right)-{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{\star}}\right)\right]}{\sqrt{T}}+\frac{16H\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}}{T}+\frac{32H\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T}+\frac{2}{T}\sum_{h=1}^{H}M_{h}^{2}\left[16L^{2}/T+4P_{h}^{2}\right].`$

Above can be rewritten as using the big-$`\mathcal{O}`$ as:

$`\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{{T}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|\nabla{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(0)}}\right)-{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{\star}}\right)}{\sqrt{T}}+\frac{\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}}{T}+\frac{\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T}+\frac{1}{T}\right).`$

Similar to Theorem 1 in wang2020tackling, the above bound convergences at a rate of $`\mathcal{O}\left(1/\sqrt{T}\right)`$ and $`\mathcal{O}\left(1/T\right)`$ for optimization term and the update noises $`\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}`$ and $`\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}`$. Similar convergence rates are also seen in other works (stich2018local; azam2021recycling).

#### Adaptive Federated Optimization (reddi2021adaptive).

Starting with the final convergence bound of Theorem [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2. ‣ E.5 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: The Main Proof ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"):

$`\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{{T}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|\nabla{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{2\left[{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(0)}}\right)-{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{\star}}\right)\right]}{{\eta}_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}{T}}+16H\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}+32H\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}`$$`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+C^{2}\frac{{\sigma}_{\mathrm{DP}}^{2}}{{\xi}^{2}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}{R}_{h}^{2}{d}_{h}+2\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}\sum_{h=1}^{H}M_{h}^{2}\left[16L^{2}\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}+\frac{1}{C_{h}^{2}}+4P_{h}^{2}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+4\frac{\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{\xi^{2}T}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\frac{R_{h}^{2}M_{h}^{2}}{C_{h}^{2}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left[\mathbb{E}_{{}_{k}}\left[\mathsf{Var}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left(\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|\right)\right]+\mathsf{Var}_{{}_{k}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|\right]\right)\right]`$

and substituting $`\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}=1/\sqrt{T}`$, $`\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}=1/\left(T^{3/4}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}\right)`$, $`\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{DP}}}^{2}=0`$ and $`C_{h}\rightarrow\infty`$, we get:

$`\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{{T}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|\nabla{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{2\left[{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(0)}}\right)-{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{\star}}\right)\right]}{(1/\sqrt{T}){T}}+16H\frac{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T^{3/2}}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}`$$`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+32H\frac{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T^{3/2}}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}+2\frac{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T^{3/2}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}M_{h}^{2}\left[16L^{2}\frac{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T^{3/2}}+4P_{h}^{2}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad=\frac{2\left[{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(0)}}\right)-{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{\star}}\right)\right]}{\sqrt{T}}+\frac{16H\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}}{T^{3/2}}+\frac{32H\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T^{3/2}}+\frac{2}{T^{3/2}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}M_{h}^{2}\left[16L^{2}T^{-3/2}+4P_{h}^{2}\right].`$

Above can be rewritten as using the big-$`\mathcal{O}`$ as:

$`\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{{T}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|\nabla{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(0)}}\right)-{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{\star}}\right)}{\sqrt{T}}+\frac{\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}}{T^{3/2}}+\frac{\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T^{3/2}}+\frac{1}{T^{3/2}}\right).`$

Similar to Corollary 1 & 2 in reddi2021adaptive, the above bound converges at a rate of $`\mathcal{O}\left(1/\sqrt{T}\right)`$ and $`\mathcal{O}\left(1/T^{3/2}\right)`$ for optimization term and the global update noise $`\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}`$ respectively. However, it follows a faster convergence rate of $`\mathcal{O}\left(1/T^{3/2}\right)`$ for the local update noise $`\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}`$ compared to a rate of $`\mathcal{O}\left(1/\sqrt{T}\right)`$ in reddi2021adaptive.

#### Understanding Gradient Clipping in Private SGD (chen2020understanding).

Starting with the final convergence bound of Theorem [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2. ‣ E.5 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: The Main Proof ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"):

$`\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{{T}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|\nabla{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{2\left[{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(0)}}\right)-{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{\star}}\right)\right]}{{\eta}_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}{T}}+16H\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}+32H\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}`$$`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{C^{2}{\sigma}_{\mathrm{DP}}^{2}}{{\xi}^{2}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}{R}_{h}^{2}{d}_{h}+2\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}\sum_{h=1}^{H}M_{h}^{2}\left[16L^{2}\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}+\frac{1}{C_{h}^{2}}+4P_{h}^{2}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+4\frac{\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{\xi^{2}T}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\frac{R_{h}^{2}M_{h}^{2}}{C_{h}^{2}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left[\mathbb{E}_{{}_{k}}\left[\mathsf{Var}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left(\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|\right)\right]+\mathsf{Var}_{{}_{k}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|\right]\right)\right]`$

and substituting $`\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}=1/\sqrt{T}`$, $`\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}=1/(\sqrt{T}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}})`$, $`\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{DP}}}^{2}=\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{\mathrm{max}_{i=1}^{K}(\omega_{i})^{2}T\ln{\frac{1}{\delta}}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)`$ (per Theorem 1 in abadi2016gaussianmoments and Lemma 1 and Theorem 1 from mcmahan2018learning to guarantee $`(\varepsilon,\delta)`$-privacy $`z^{2}\geq const\frac{q^{2}T\log{1/\delta}}{\varepsilon^{2}}`$, while $`\sigma_{\mathrm{DP}}=z\cdot\mathbb{S}=z\cdot\mathrm{max}_{i=1}^{K}\omega_{i}/q`$), $`R_{h}=1`$, $`C_{h}=\frac{C}{\sqrt{H}}`$, and $`D=\sum_{h=1}^{H}{d}_{h}`$, we get,

$`\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{{T}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|\nabla{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{2\left[{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(0)}}\right)-{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{\star}}\right)\right]}{(1/\sqrt{T}){T}}+16H\frac{1}{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}\frac{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}`$$`\displaystyle\qquad+32H\frac{1}{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}\frac{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}+\frac{1}{{\xi}^{2}}\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{C^{2}\mathrm{max}_{i=1}^{K}(\omega_{i})^{2}T\ln{\frac{1}{\delta}}}{\varepsilon^{2}}\right)\sum_{h=1}^{H}{d}_{h}`$$`\displaystyle\qquad+2\frac{1}{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}\frac{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T}\sum_{h=1}^{H}M_{h}^{2}\left[16L^{2}\frac{1}{TT_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}+\frac{H}{C^{2}}+4P_{h}^{2}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\qquad+4\frac{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{\xi^{2}T^{2}}\frac{1}{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\frac{HR_{h}^{2}M_{h}^{2}}{C^{2}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left[\mathbb{E}_{{}_{k}}\left[\mathsf{Var}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left(\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|\right)\right]+\mathsf{Var}_{{}_{k}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|\right]\right)\right]`$$`\displaystyle\quad\overset{}{=}\frac{2\left[{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(0)}}\right)-{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{\star}}\right)\right]}{\sqrt{T}}+\frac{16H\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}}{T}+\frac{32H\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T}+\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{DC^{2}\mathrm{max}_{i=1}^{K}(\omega_{i})^{2}T\ln{\frac{1}{\delta}}}{{\xi}^{2}\varepsilon^{2}}\right)`$$`\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{2}{T}\sum_{h=1}^{H}M_{h}^{2}\left[16\frac{L^{2}}{T}+\frac{H}{C^{2}}+4P_{h}^{2}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{4}{\xi^{2}T^{2}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\frac{HR_{h}^{2}M_{h}^{2}}{C^{2}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left[\mathbb{E}_{{}_{k}}\left[\mathsf{Var}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left(\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|\right)\right]+\mathsf{Var}_{{}_{k}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|\right]\right)\right].`$

Using the big-$`\mathcal{O}`$, above can be rewritten as

$`\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{{T}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|\nabla{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{O}\left(\frac{{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(0)}}\right)-{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{\star}}\right)}{\sqrt{T}}+\frac{\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}}{T}+\frac{\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{T}+\frac{DC^{2}\mathrm{max}_{i=1}^{K}(\omega_{i})^{2}T\ln{\frac{1}{\delta}}}{\varepsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{T}+\frac{1}{C^{2}T}\right).`$

By setting $`C=T^{-1/4}`$, a similar convergence bound can be recovered up to a constant from Theorem 3.1 in chen2020understanding by choosing $`\eta_{g}=1/T^{1/4}`$, $`\eta_{l}=1/(T^{1/4}Q)`$, $`C=\eta_{l}Q`$ ($`Q`$ being analogous to $`T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}`$ in our work), and $`P=1`$ ($`P`$ is analogous to $`q`$, i.e., client sampling proportion in our work) and $`\omega_{i}=1/K`$, though our bound has better rate of convergence for global and local update noise $`\mathcal{O}\left(1/T\right)`$ compared to $`\mathcal{O}\left(1/\sqrt{T}\right)`$ in chen2020understanding.

#### Inverse Relationship to Clipping Constant.

While chen2020understanding analyzes clipping it does not highlight an inverse relationship with clipping constant $`C`$ as seen in our work. Similar inverse relationships have also been highlighted in central optimizer analysis (koloskova2023revisiting).

### E.8 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: Theorem Under Limited Participation

Estimator with Bounded Sensitivity for FL with DP

It is common in several FL works (wang2020tackling; hosseinalipour2020multi; li2019convergence; azam2021recycling) to use weighted averaging of client updates given by:

``` math
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{K}^{t}|}{\omega}_{k_{i}}\right)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{K}^{t}|}{\omega}_{k_{i}}\left({\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k_{i}}}^{{(t)}}+{\mathbf{z}}_{{h,k_{i}}}^{{(t)}}\right),
```

where $`\mathcal{K}^{t}`$ is a set of sampled users, $`\omega_{s}=\frac{\left|\mathcal{D}_{s}\right|}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\left|\mathcal{D}_{k}\right|}`$ and $`\left|\mathcal{D}_{s}\right|`$ represents the cardinality of the data on client $`s`$. As discussed in prior work on moments accountant for DP (mcmahan2018learning), this estimator does not have a bounded sensitivity, thus ineligible for guaranteed DP privacy. The unbounded sensitivity can be intuitively seen via the case where all the sampled clients $`\mathcal{K}^{t}`$ have low number of data points thus leading to an explosion of the term $`\left(\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{K}^{t}|}{\omega}_{k_{i}}\right)^{-1}`$. Because of this, our analysis uses the unbiased sampling estimator from mcmahan2018learning which can be expressed as

``` math
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}=\frac{1}{q}\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{K}^{t}|}{\omega}_{k_{i}}\left({\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k_{i}}}^{{(t)}}+{\mathbf{z}}_{{h,k_{i}}}^{{(t)}}\right)=\frac{1}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}\left({\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}+{\mathbf{z}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right),
```

where $`q=S/K`$, users are sampled i.i.d. with probability $`q`$ from the population $`K`$ and $`{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(q)`$ with $`\mathbb{E}\left[{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}\right]=q`$. It can be seen that the “unbiasedness” of the estimator results from the fact:

``` math
\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{K}^{t}|}{\omega}_{k_{i}}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}\right]=\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}\mathbb{E}\left[{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}\right]=q\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}=q.
```

Also, while our algorithm and analysis use this general form of unbiased sampling estimator in Equation [E.65](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A5.E65 "Equation E.65 ‣ E.8 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: Theorem Under Limited Participation ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"), our simulation experiments use uniform averaging with $`\omega_{k}=1/K`$.

###### Theorem 3.

Assume [A1.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A5.Ex1 "Equation A1.1 ‣ Item 1 ‣ E.1 Assumptions ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"), [A2.1](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A5.Ex3 "Equation A2.1 ‣ Item 2 ‣ E.1 Assumptions ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"), [A2.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A5.Ex4 "Equation A2.2 ‣ Item 2 ‣ E.1 Assumptions ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"), and [A3](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A5.Ex5 "Equation A3 ‣ Item 3 ‣ E.1 Assumptions ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"), $`{\eta}_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}L<1`$ and $`\kappa=\left[1-10(1-\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}})^{2}\right]>0`$. If the trust ratio from Eq. [E.9](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A5.E9 "Equation E.9 ‣ E.4 LAMB ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") in $`\mathrm{LAMB}`$ optimizer is controlled in the Algorithm [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ FL with Differential Privacy (DP) ‣ 2 Federated Learning with Differential Privacy: Background and Notation ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") (global optimizer is $`\mathrm{LAMB}`$ and local optimizer is SGD) such that $`{r}_{h}^{(t)}\leq{R}_{h}`$ and $`\left\|{\mathbf{1}}-{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\right\|_{\infty}\leq{P}_{h}`$, $`\beta_{1}=0`$ and $`\lambda=0`$ in $`\mathrm{LAMB}`$ optimizer, and clients are i.i.d. sampled with probability $`q`$, then after $`{T}`$ steps of aggregation the performance of FL with DP, per-layer clipping and layer-wise gradient normalization is characterized by the following upper bound:

$`\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{{T}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|\nabla{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{2\left[{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(0)}}\right)-{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{\star}}\right)\right]}{{\eta}_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}{T}}+20H\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}+40H\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}`$$`\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C^{2}{\sigma}_{\mathrm{DP}}^{2}}{{\xi}^{2}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}{R}_{h}^{2}{d}_{h}+5\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}\sum_{h=1}^{H}M_{h}^{2}\left[8L^{2}\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2qC_{h}^{2}}+\frac{P_{h}^{2}}{q}+\frac{1-q}{q}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\qquad+5\frac{\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{q\xi^{2}T}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\frac{R_{h}^{2}M_{h}^{2}}{C_{h}^{2}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left[\mathbb{E}_{{}_{k}}\left[\mathsf{Var}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left(\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|\right)\right]+\mathsf{Var}_{{}_{k}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|\right]\right)\right]`$

where $`k\sim\mathrm{Categorical}(\omega_{1},\dots,\omega_{K})`$ and $`\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\cdot\right]`$ denotes the expectation over sampled mini-batch $`\mathscr{B}_{k}^{(t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}})}`$ every local step $`t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}=1,\dots,T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}`$ from the client data: $`\boldsymbol{x}^{(t,t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}})}_{k}\sim\mathcal{D}_{k}`$, $`\boldsymbol{x}^{(t,t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}})}_{k}\in\mathscr{B}_{k}^{(t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}})}`$, $`|\mathscr{B}_{k}^{(t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}})}|=B_{k}`$.

###### Proof.

Using the unbiased sampling estimator from Equation [E.65](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A5.E65 "Equation E.65 ‣ E.8 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: Theorem Under Limited Participation ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") we start by bounding $`{\mathbf{Z}}_{{h}}`$.

Bounding $`{\mathbf{Z}}_{{h}}`$ Under Client Sampling

Under client sampling we have the aggregated clients updates $`{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}`$ in Equation [E.14](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A5.E14 "Equation E.14 ‣ E.5 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: The Main Proof ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") defined as:

``` math
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}=\frac{1}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}\left({\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}+{\mathbf{z}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right),
```

where $`q<<1`$ in usual scenario and $`{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(q)`$. The definition of $`{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}`$ in Equation [E.68](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A5.E68 "Equation E.68 ‣ E.8 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: Theorem Under Limited Participation ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") thus affects the bound on $`{\mathbf{Z}}_{{h}}`$ (from Equation [E.27](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A5.E27 "Equation E.27 ‣ E.5 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: The Main Proof ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping")) as follows:

$`\displaystyle{{\mathbf{Z}}_{{h}}}`$$`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}},{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}}\Bigg[\bigg\|\nabla{\mathscr{L}}_{h}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\right)`$$`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}+\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}`$$`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}\gamma_{k}{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}+\frac{1}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}\gamma_{k}{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}`$$`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad-\frac{1}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\odot{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}-\frac{1}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\odot{\mathbf{z}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}`$$`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\odot\widetilde{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}-\frac{1}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\odot\widetilde{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}`$$`\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{1}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\odot\overline{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}-\frac{1}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\odot\overline{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\bigg\|^{2}\Bigg].`$

Bounding Term with DP Noise in $`{{\mathbf{Z}}_{{h}}}`$ Under Client Sampling

Having upper bound on the expectation $`\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}},{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}}}\left[{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\right]_{i}^{2}\leq\frac{R_{h}^{2}}{\xi^{2}}`$ and random independent DP noise $`{\mathbf{z}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,{\mathbf{I}}_{{h}}C^{2}{\sigma}_{{}_{\mathrm{DP}}}^{2}\frac{q}{\sum_{i=1}^{K}w_{i}^{2}}\right)`$ per theorem condition (thus $`{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}`$ and $`{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}`$ are independent variables), let’s get the upper bound first for:

$`\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}},{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}}}\left[\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\odot{\mathbf{z}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}},{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}}}\left[\left\|{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\odot\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}{\mathbf{z}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|^{2}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{h}}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}},{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}}}\left[\left[{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\right]_{i}^{2}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}{\mathbf{z}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right]^{2}_{i}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{h}}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\right]_{i}^{2}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}},{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}}}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}{\mathbf{z}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right]_{i}^{2}\leq\frac{R_{h}^{2}}{\xi^{2}}d_{h}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}},{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}}}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}{\mathbf{z}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right]_{0}^{2}`$$`\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{R_{h}^{2}}{\xi^{2}}d_{h}C^{2}{\sigma}_{{}_{\mathrm{DP}}}^{2}\frac{q}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{2}}\,\sum_{k=1}^{K}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}}}\left[{\omega}_{k}^{2}({\gamma}_{k}^{(t)})^{2}\right]=\frac{R_{h}^{2}}{\xi^{2}}d_{h}C^{2}{\sigma}_{{}_{\mathrm{DP}}}^{2}q^{2}.`$

Bounding $`{{\mathbf{Z}}_{{h}}}`$ with $`{\mathbf{Y}}_{{1}}`$, $`{\mathbf{Y}}_{{2}}`$, $`{\mathbf{Y}}_{{3}}`$, $`{\mathbf{Y}}_{{4}}`$ and $`{\mathbf{W}}`$ Terms Under Client Sampling

Given Equations [E.28](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A5.E28 "Equation E.28 ‣ E.5 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: The Main Proof ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"), [E.32](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A5.E32 "Equation E.32 ‣ E.5 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: The Main Proof ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"), [E.29](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A5.E29 "Equation E.29 ‣ E.5 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: The Main Proof ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") and [E.30](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A5.E30 "Equation E.30 ‣ E.5 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: The Main Proof ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") (we use the fact that DP noise $`{\mathbf{z}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}`$ is zero-mean independent variable), we can bound $`{\mathbf{Z}}_{{h}}`$ in the following way:

$`\displaystyle{\mathbf{Z}}_{{h}}`$$`\displaystyle\leq 5\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}\left\|\nabla{\mathscr{L}}_{h}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\right)-{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|^{2}\right]}_{{\mathbf{Y}}_{{1}}}`$$`\displaystyle\quad+5\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}},{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}}}\left[\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}-\frac{1}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|^{2}\right]}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{0,0,1}{\mathbf{W}}}`$$`\displaystyle\quad+5\frac{1}{q^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}},{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}}}\left[\left\|{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}\left({\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}-{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\odot\overline{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,s}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\quad+5\frac{1}{q^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}},{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}}}\left[\left\|{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\odot\left({\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\quad+5\frac{1}{q^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}},{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}}}\left[\left\|{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\odot\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}-\overline{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]+\frac{{R}_{h}^{2}}{{\xi}^{2}}{d}_{h}C^{2}{\sigma}_{\mathrm{DP}}^{2}`$$`\displaystyle\leq\left\{\mathbb{E}\left[{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}\right]^{2}=q;{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}\text{ is independent variable from the gradients and deltas}\right\}`$$`\displaystyle\leq 5\mathbf{Y_{1}}+5\mathbf{W}+5\frac{1}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|\left({\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}-{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\odot\overline{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,s}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]}_{{\mathbf{Y}}_{{2}}}`$$`\displaystyle\quad+5\frac{1}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\odot\left({\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}-\widetilde{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]}_{{\mathbf{Y}}_{{3}}}`$$`\displaystyle\quad+5\frac{1}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|{\mathbf{p}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\odot\left(\widetilde{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}-\overline{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]}_{{\mathbf{Y}}_{{4}}}+\frac{{R}_{h}^{2}}{{\xi}^{2}}{d}_{h}C^{2}{\sigma}_{\mathrm{DP}}^{2}.`$

$`\displaystyle\mathbf{W}`$$`\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}},{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}}}\left[\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}\left({\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}-\frac{1}{q}{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\overset{\text{ Eq.\penalty 10000\ \ref{eq:jensens}}}{\leq}\frac{1}{q^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}},{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}}}\left[\left\|(q-{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}){\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|^{2}\right]`$$`\displaystyle=\frac{1}{q^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}}}\left[\left(q-{\gamma}_{k}^{(t)}\right)^{2}\right]\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|^{2}\right]=\frac{1-q}{q}\sum_{k=1}^{K}{\omega}_{k}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|^{2}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\overset{\text{ Eq.\penalty 10000\ \ref{eq:Gnorm-bound}}}{\leq}\frac{1-q}{q}{\eta}_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}{T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}^{2}{M}_{h}^{2}`$

Reusing bounds for $`{\mathbf{Y}}_{{1}}`$, $`{\mathbf{Y}}_{{2}}`$, $`{\mathbf{Y}}_{{3}}`$, $`{\mathbf{Y}}_{{4}}`$ from the proof of Theorem [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2. ‣ E.5 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: The Main Proof ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") and having $`\kappa=\left[1-10(1-\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}})^{2}\right]`$, we get:

$`\displaystyle\frac{\kappa}{{T}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|\nabla{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|^{2}\right]\leq\frac{2\left[{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{(0)}}\right)-{\mathscr{L}}\left({\mathbf{\boldsymbol{\theta}}}^{{\star}}\right)\right]}{{\eta}_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}{T}}+20H\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{glob}}}^{2}+40H\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}\sigma_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}`$ $`\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C^{2}{\sigma}_{\mathrm{DP}}^{2}}{{\xi}^{2}}\sum_{h=1}^{H}{R}_{h}^{2}{d}_{h}+5\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}\sum_{h=1}^{H}M_{h}^{2}\left[8L^{2}\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2qC_{h}^{2}}+\frac{P_{h}^{2}}{q}+\frac{1-q}{q}\right]`$$`\displaystyle\qquad+5\frac{\eta_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}T_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}^{2}}{q\xi^{2}T}\sum_{h=1}^{H}\frac{R_{h}^{2}M_{h}^{2}}{C_{h}^{2}}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\left[\mathbb{E}_{{}_{k}}\left[\mathsf{Var}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left(\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|\right)\right]+\mathsf{Var}_{{}_{k}}\left(\mathbb{E}_{{}_{t_{{}_{\mathrm{loc}}}}}\left[\left\|{\mathbf{G}}_{{h,k}}^{{(t)}}\right\|\right]\right)\right].`$

∎

Another Estimator for Limited Participation

In prior work (mcmahan2018learning) it was shown that another estimator can be used for weighted averaging of client updates under client sampling:

``` math
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{max}(q_{\mathrm{min}},\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{K}^{t}|}{\omega}_{k_{i}})}\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{K}^{t}|}{\omega}_{k_{i}}\left({\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k_{i}}}^{{(t)}}+{\mathbf{z}}_{{h,k_{i}}}^{{(t)}}\right),
```

This estimator is not unbiased compared to the one we use in Algorithm [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ FL with Differential Privacy (DP) ‣ 2 Federated Learning with Differential Privacy: Background and Notation ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") and mcmahan2018learning gives the differential privacy guarantees for it too. To obtain the convergence bound for this estimator similar to Theorems [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 2. ‣ E.5 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: The Main Proof ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") and [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 3. ‣ E.8 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: Theorem Under Limited Participation ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") we can use the fact that ($`q_{\mathrm{min}}\leq q`$)

``` math
\left\|{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h}}^{{(t)}}\right\|\leq\frac{1}{q_{\mathrm{min}}}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{|\mathcal{K}^{t}|}{\omega}_{k_{i}}\left({\mathbf{\Delta}}_{{h,k_{i}}}^{{(t)}}+{\mathbf{z}}_{{h,k_{i}}}^{{(t)}}\right)\right\|.
```

Having this bound we can repeat the same steps as we did for the unbiased estimator from Algorithm [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ FL with Differential Privacy (DP) ‣ 2 Federated Learning with Differential Privacy: Background and Notation ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") and get similar asymptotic bound as in Theorem [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#Thmtheorem3 "Theorem 3. ‣ E.8 Adaptive Optimizers and Per-Layer Clipping: Theorem Under Limited Participation ‣ Appendix E Theoretical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") but with change of $`q`$ to $`q_{\mathrm{min}}`$ and different sensitivity bound for DP noise.

## Appendix F Empirical Analysis: Data and Central Models Training

#### Data

We perform all experiments using two datasets of audio-transcription pairs: LibriSpeech (panayotov2015librispeech) and Common Voice v13.0 (ardila2020common). These two datasets are read speech but differ in other properties, like data diversity, noise conditions, speaker variation, and speaker distribution. We not only present results with English locale in LibriSpeech and Common Voice v13.0 but also complement them with results on French and German locale from Common Voice v13.0. For LibriSpeech data, the original 16kHz sampling rate is maintained, while for Common Voice we downsampled every audio to 16kHz sampling rate.

Every split of LS and CV has a separate set of speakers as well as every validation and test sets have entirely different speakers from the train. Validation data are used to tune all hyper-parameters and to select the best models based on the word error rate (WER), while the test sets are used only for final evaluation. Statistics on the number of speakers and the number of minutes per speaker are given in Figure [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#S4.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ Central Training ‣ 4 Empirical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") for both LS and CV datasets and their subsets. The statistics show that CV data are much more heterogeneous than LS as highlighted by gao2022e2easr. CV data thus enable a more realistic scenario for testing FL and FL with DP. The most realistic scenario for FL uses a small central dataset to train a seed model (e.g. LS-100), and a larger dataset from a different distribution for FL (e.g. CV-en-train). All training subsets used in the empirical analysis and their statistics are listed in Table [2](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A6.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Training ‣ Appendix F Empirical Analysis: Data and Central Models Training ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping").

#### Token Set

likhomanenko2020rethinking showed that for data from different domains, character tokens are more suited than word-pieces. Since in this paper we consider settings with data from different domains, the token set used in all our experiments is composed of English characters (a-z), augmented with a word boundary token, hyphen and apostrophe, resulting in a total of 29 characters. For French and German, common non-English characters are included as well.

#### Data preprocessing

For CV English, transcriptions are normalized similarly as for LS by (i) lower casing; (ii) removing punctuation while preserving hyphen; and (iii) converting non-English characters into English ones with unidecode⁵⁵5[https://pypi.org/project/Unidecode](https://pypi.org/project/Unidecode). package. For CV French and German, we do not remove non-English characters and we retain single quotes.

#### Model

We start our experimentation with the state-of-the-art model on LS-100 from likhomanenko2021slimipl: (i) 1D convolution to perform striding (kernel of 7 with stride of 3); (ii) a transformer encoder with 36 layers, post-LayerNorm, 4 attention heads, an embedding dimension of 768, an MLP dimension of 3072, a dropout and layer drop (fan2019reducing) of 0.3; and (iii) a linear layer to map to the target vocabulary. The resulting model has 255M trainable parameters. We focus only on a CTC model as it contains only the encoder part, is simpler to train in practice compared to Seq2Seq or Transducer models, and is less likely to over-fit to the language model (synnaeve2020endtoend).

#### Positional Embedding

To reduce model training time by a factor of approximately $`2`$-$`3`$ and to reduce the memory footprint, we use CAPE positional embedding (likhomanenko2021cape) instead of relative positional embedding (shaw2018self); both models perform similarly.

#### SpecAugment

SpecAugment (park2019specaugment) is activated from the very first step of training. Two frequency masks with frequency mask parameter $`F=30`$, ten time masks with maximum time-mask ratio $`p=0.1`$ and time mask parameter $`T=50`$ are used; time warping is not used.

#### Training

We train models on 8 GPUs (A100 80GB), and use a dynamic batch size of $`\sim 240`$s audio per GPU. For all central models training, we use LARS optimizer with the learning rate of 0.5 (for models fine-tuned from seed models trained on CV-\*-train-10 we use 0.2) without a warmup period. Training is done for up to 300k-600k steps until full convergence with step-wise (by 2x) learning rate decay every 50k steps started at 40k-330k depending on the model.

## Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy

### G.1 Hyper-parameters

All dropout and layer drop are fixed to 0.3 We train each client with a dynamic batch size of total $`120`$s of audio (CV) or $`360`$s of audio (LS). In Figures [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#S4.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 4.1 Impact of Seed Models and Cohort Size ‣ 4 Empirical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") and [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 4.1 Impact of Seed Models and Cohort Size ‣ 4 Empirical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") we use the same LR and LR decay schedule for all seed models regardless of the cohort size or the data used to train a seed model. Optimal hyper-parameters (e.g. LR) are likely to depend on the quality of the seed model and cohort size. Thus, the results could likely be further improved by tuning the LR and its decay schedule for each cohort size and seed model separately. Furthermore, we can improve models by longer training exceeding 2k central steps as shown in ablations in Appendix [G.8](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.SS8 "G.8 Extending the Number of Central FL Iterations ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"), Table [14](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T14 "Table 14 ‣ G.8 Extending the Number of Central FL Iterations ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping").

### G.2 Detailed Results for English

Table [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T3 "Table 3 ‣ G.2 Detailed Results for English ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") details the results for LS from Figure [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#S4.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ 4.1 Impact of Seed Models and Cohort Size ‣ 4 Empirical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") and Table [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T4 "Table 4 ‣ G.2 Detailed Results for English ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") details the results for CV from Figure [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 4.1 Impact of Seed Models and Cohort Size ‣ 4 Empirical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"). ### G.3 Impact of Model Architecture on FL Performance in ASR

Table [7](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T7 "Table 7 ‣ G.3 Impact of Model Architecture on FL Performance in ASR ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") compares several model architectures for the trivial FL scenario with cohort size 1 and 64k central iterations on LS-100. Cohort size of 1 is impractical but it eliminates the impact of federated averaging. The learning rates and learning rate decay schedules are tuned for each architecture. During preliminary FL experiments we have observed that pre-LayerNorm models often perform better than post-LayerNorm ones. It is of note that without a linear central learning rate warmup, we were unable to train reasonable FL models with post-LayerNorm. Our experiments showed that FL models with pre-LayerNorm are easier to train, they do not require a central learning rate warmup, and they are generally more robust with respect to hyper-parameters. These observations are similar to prior works on transformers central training (zhang2022understanding; zhai2023stabilizing). That is why we use the pre-LayerNorm configuration for all experiments in the paper. It is interesting that for this trivial FL scenario FL models outperforms centrally trained models. However, when we switch to larger LS-960 dataset, this does not hold anymore.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Model | Warmup | dev-clean | dev-other | test-clean | test-other |
| Central pre-LayerNorm | 0 | $`\numprint{5.9}`$ | $`\numprint{18.9}`$ | $`\numprint{6.4}`$ | $`\numprint{19.2}`$ |
| FL pre-LayerNorm | 0 | $`\numprint{5.55}`$ | $`\numprint{17.65}`$ | $`\numprint{5.94}`$ | $`\numprint{17.93}`$ |
| Central post-LayerNorm | 0 | $`\numprint{8.1}`$ | $`\numprint{25.0}`$ | $`\numprint{8.6}`$ | $`\numprint{25.6}`$ |
| FL post-LayerNorm | 1000 | $`\numprint{5.91}`$ | $`\numprint{17.45}`$ | $`\numprint{6.26}`$ | $`\numprint{17.96}`$ |

Table 7: Comparison (WER, %) between pre-LayerNorm and post-LayerNorm architectures in transformer for trivial FL scenario with cohort size $`S=1`$ and central steps $`T=64`$k on LS-100. pre-LayerNorm models perform best and their training is robust with respect to hyper-parameters such as the learning schedule. Central models are trained according to Appendix [F](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A6 "Appendix F Empirical Analysis: Data and Central Models Training ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"). FL models use exponential learning rate decay, LAMB as central and SGD as local optimizers.

Table 9: Comparison (WER, %) of various optimizers on CV-en with and wihout seed models. For LAMB, the results and parameters are the same as those in Table [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T4 "Table 4 ‣ G.2 Detailed Results for English ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") (note that these are sub-optimal because for simplicity we use the same learning rate and learning rate decay schedule for each configuration regardless of the cohort size and all runs with seed models use the same configuration). For all other optimizers, the central learning rate and the learning rate decay schedule are tuned separately for each combination of cohort size and seed model.

Table [9](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T9 "Table 9 ‣ G.4 Impact of Server Optimizer on FL Performance in ASR ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") compares LAMB with Adam, AdaGrad (duchi2012adagrad), LARS, and SGD (sutskever2013sgd) on several configurations for CV-en dataset. The results on CV show that without seed models, LAMB performs significantly better than all other optimizers but with seed models, LAMB is sometimes outperformed slightly by LARS and Adam. SGD, AdaGrad and Adam are outperformed by LAMB and LARS in almost all scenarios.

During hyper-parameter tuning, some adaptive optimizers (e.g., Adam) often became unstable and the training diverged, especially without a well performing seed model. Furthermore, the optimal parameters of these optimizers oftentimes vary significantly between, e.g., the cohort sizes, indicating that they are less robust than LAMB in our setting.

The robustness of LAMB across all scenarios and its stability are the main reasons for choosing LAMB as the central optimizer for most of the experiments in the paper. However, the results in Table [9](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T9 "Table 9 ‣ G.4 Impact of Server Optimizer on FL Performance in ASR ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") suggest that some of the models could be further improved with more hyper-parameters tuning and choosing the best optimizer for each case. Also, azam2023fl_asr showed that tuning other optimizer parameters, e.g. $`\varepsilon`$ in Adam, can significantly improve FL model training for ASR. However, in this paper we restrict ourselves to tuning only the learning rate and learning rate schedule; the remaining parameters were set to their default values from optax library⁶⁶6[https://optax.readthedocs.io/en/latest/](https://optax.readthedocs.io/en/latest/).

We have not completed an extensive evaluation of other optimizers for local training to keep it efficient (no state, no additional memory, no extra computations): SGD as a local optimizer is robust and efficient in our experiments. However, preliminary experiments show that LARS and LAMB are well suited candidates for replacing SGD as the local optimizer and will likely outperform SGD.

For completeness, here we provide more details on optimizer tuning. For both LS-960 and CV-en-train without a seed model, we tuned the central LR for LAMB between $`0.001`$ and $`0.009`$, and the local LR for SGD from $`0.2`$ to $`0.6`$. We have done the same for one selected seed model for each dataset. Additionally, we tried several learning rate schedules, including constant rate, step decay, and exponential decay on several configurations. After the initial experiments, we chose one configuration for each dataset (LS, CV) without a seed model and one configuration for each dataset (LS, CV) with a seed model, and we ran the remaining experiments with the chosen configurations. The initial tuning was done on smaller cohort sizes. For other optimizers discussed in this section, we tuned the key parameters until a locally optimal value was found for central LR for each presented experiment, and we considered 4 variations of the exponential decay rate for each LR value.

Table 10: Results (WER, %) on CV for English, French and German. Configurations are identical to those in Figure [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 4.1 Impact of Seed Models and Cohort Size ‣ 4 Empirical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") and Table [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T4 "Table 4 ‣ G.2 Detailed Results for English ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") regardless of the language.

Table [10](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T10 "Table 10 ‣ G.5 Detailed Results for CV French and German ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") shows the results of FL on CV for French and German languages, and for comparison it provides the corresponding results on CV for English. To demonstrate that the settings used for English language were robust, we did not tune any parameters for French and German, and simply used the exact same configuration that was used in the corresponding training on English language.

The results show that even though French and German have considerably smaller datasets, the training is apparently considerably easier and WERs are significantly smaller than for English whether or not a seed model is used. This is likely due to the degree of consistency between the orthography and phonology as was discussed for example in borgwaldt2004langentropy; borleffs2017orthocomplexity; ziegler1996StatisticalAO; sprenger_charolles2003orthotophono; German and French have stronger orthography-to-phonology consistency than English. Furthermore, the results for French are considerably better than those presented by gao2022e2easr. As French and German data are smaller, for the same cohort size and central steps we do more epochs over data for French and German than for English CV. Thus, FL training can match the central training with smaller cohort size for both French and German compared to English. It is of note that French and German turn out to be easier also for FL with DP as shown in Appendix [H.6](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A8.SS6 "H.6 Federated Learning with Differential Privacy for French and German ‣ Appendix H Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning with Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"), Table [18](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A8.T18 "Table 18 ‣ H.6 Federated Learning with Differential Privacy for French and German ‣ Appendix H Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning with Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping").

Configurations are identical to those in Figure [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 4.1 Impact of Seed Models and Cohort Size ‣ 4 Empirical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") and Table [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T4 "Table 4 ‣ G.2 Detailed Results for English ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") except the SpecAugment schedule as noted in the table.

In all experiments so far, we used SpecAugment (park2019specaugment) activated from the very first step of training as was also common in most prior works. Table [11](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T11 "Table 11 ‣ G.6 Impact of SpecAugment ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") shows the results with and without SpecAugment for several configurations analyzed in this paper on LS data. These results confirm that SpecAugment improves WER in all the cases.

However, Table [12](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T12 "Table 12 ‣ G.6 Impact of SpecAugment ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") shows that SpecAugment appears to have a negative impact on the trained models for CV (English), especially for FL training without a seed model and small cohort sizes. This is surprising as prior works reported only improved results with SpecAugment for transformer models. These results also reveal another difference between benchmarks on LS and on CV.

It is possible that the results with SpecAugment on CV would improve if SpecAugment was turned on later in the training and its parameters were tuned for each scenario separately. Nonetheless, since in most scenarios SpecAugment either improved models or the differences were marginal, for simplicity, we use SpecAugment in all experiments in this paper.

### G.7 Performance of FedProx in FL for ASR

li2020fedprox proposed FedProx to alleviate the impact of heterogeneous data on FL performance. Since the results presented earlier in Tables [5](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T5 "Table 5 ‣ G.2 Detailed Results for English ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") and [6](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T6 "Table 6 ‣ G.2 Detailed Results for English ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") suggested that heterogeneous data pose a challenge for FL also in our training, we also evaluate the impact of FedProx on model quality in ASR. For each configuration, we use FedProx with the regularization weight $`\mu\in\{0.00001,0.0001,0.001,0.01,0.1,1.0\}`$ and chose the best result, as suggested by li2020fedprox.

Table [13](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T13 "Table 13 ‣ G.7 Performance of FedProx in FL for ASR ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") presents the results of using FedProx in several scenarios on LS and CV datasets presented earlier in Tables [3](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T3 "Table 3 ‣ G.2 Detailed Results for English ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") and [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T4 "Table 4 ‣ G.2 Detailed Results for English ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"). The results show FedProx improves model performance (WER is decreased) in 8 out of 10 training configurations tested, although in most cases the improvement is marginal. In one of the remaining cases there is no change and only in one case the results with FedProx are considerably worse than without it.

It is surprising how the optimal value of the key FedProx parameter $`\mu`$ changes considerably between the various scenarios. This suggests that it would make sense to evaluate adaptive $`\mu`$ as suggested by li2020fedprox. We leave the use of adaptive $`\mu`$ and the investigation of how FedProx may improve FL training robustness (e.g. with respect to the number of local epochs or steps) for future work.

We also tried limiting the number of batches processed on each client (wang2020tackling) and normalizing users’ deltas sent to the server (charles2021large) but neither approach improved the results. See Table [7](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A8.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ H.2 Large Cohort Training Implementation ‣ Appendix H Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning with Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") in Appendix [H.2](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A8.SS2 "H.2 Large Cohort Training Implementation ‣ Appendix H Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning with Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") for the results on limiting the number of batches (steps) processed for each client.

### G.8 Extending the Number of Central FL Iterations

Table [14](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T14 "Table 14 ‣ G.8 Extending the Number of Central FL Iterations ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") shows that even though most FL models were stopped after 2k central steps, letting these models to train longer would further improve performance. However, due to the communication complexity for each central step, it is best to use a moderate number of central steps and maximize utility of the training by optimizing the parameters for local, on-device training, cohort sizes, and other key FL parameters.

### G.10 Scaling to Larger Cohorts

We further scale the cohort size to check limitations on the cohort size and scaling of FL: for efficient GPU utilization we switch from 10 local epochs to 10 local steps for cohort sizes of 256 to 5120, while keeping all other hyper-parameters the same. Results are shown in Table [16](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A7.T16 "Table 16 ‣ G.10 Scaling to Larger Cohorts ‣ Appendix G Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning without Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"): FL scales to larger cohort sizes lowering further WER. There is also observed degradation by switching from local epochs to local steps especially for a stronger seed model, likely due to overfitting to the seed model’s data, which are out-of-domain data with respect to the FL data.

## Appendix H Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning with Differential Privacy

### H.1 Differential Privacy Noise Discussion

There are different equivalent formulations how the noise can be added to the clients’ deltas to introduce DP, which can cause confusion about the noise scale, and how the moments accountant is applied. Having Algorithm [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ FL with Differential Privacy (DP) ‣ 2 Federated Learning with Differential Privacy: Background and Notation ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"), step [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ FL with Differential Privacy (DP) ‣ 2 Federated Learning with Differential Privacy: Background and Notation ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") can be defined as follows:

1.  1.
    Noise is added on the client level: $`\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{t}=1/q\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}^{t}}\omega_{k}\left[\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{k}^{t}+{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.3}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.3,0.3}\mathcal{N}(0,IC^{2}\sigma_{client}^{2})}\right]`$. We use this definition with $`\sigma_{client}=\sigma\cdot\sqrt{\frac{q}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{2}}}`$. It was also used by zhang2022understanding.
2.  2.
    Noise is added on the server level after averaging clients’ deltas: $`\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{t}=1/q\left[\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}^{t}}\omega_{k}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{k}^{t}\right]+{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.3}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.3,0.3}\mathcal{N}(0,IC^{2}\sigma_{avg}^{2})}`$. This is the definition used by mcmahan2018learning.
3.  3.
    Noise is added on the server level after summation but before normalization to the number of clients (used by abadi2016gaussianmoments): $`\boldsymbol{\Delta}^{t}=1/(qK)\left[\left(\sum_{k\in\mathcal{K}^{t}}K\omega_{k}\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{k}^{t}\right)+{\color[rgb]{1,0.3,0.3}\definecolor[named]{pgfstrokecolor}{rgb}{1,0.3,0.3}\mathcal{N}(0,IC^{2}\sigma_{sum}^{2})}\right]`$.

Different variants of noise are connected with each other via $`\sigma_{sum}=\sigma_{avg}\cdot qK=\sigma_{avg}\cdot S`$ and $`\sigma_{client}=\sigma_{avg}\cdot\sqrt{\frac{q}{\sum_{k=1}^{K}\omega_{k}^{2}}}`$. Then we can compute that $`\sigma_{\mathrm{DP}}=\sigma_{avg}`$ in this notation from Algorithm [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ FL with Differential Privacy (DP) ‣ 2 Federated Learning with Differential Privacy: Background and Notation ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping").

Throughout the paper we use $`\omega_{k}=\frac{1}{K}`$ and the moments accountant implementation from opacus (opacus) which works with $`\sigma_{sum}`$ noise definition. To re-scale noise added to each client in order to be consistent with opacus, we re-scale it by multiplying by the cohort size $`S`$. Thus, we get Theorem [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 1. ‣ FL with Differential Privacy (DP) ‣ 2 Federated Learning with Differential Privacy: Background and Notation ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") where $`z`$ is defined as $`z=\sigma_{sum}`$ and, finally, we get the bound on $`\sigma_{\mathrm{DP}}`$ via Theorem 1 from abadi2016gaussianmoments which gives bound on $`z^{2}\geq const\frac{q^{2}T\log{1/\delta}}{\varepsilon^{2}}`$ in our notation. In all experiments with FL with DP, we use the same privacy budget for every training step.

### H.2 Large Cohort Training Implementation

Our initial FL implementation processed the clients in each cohort sequentially, potentially parallelizing the training for each client using multiple GPUs. For each client, we train a local model for a given number of epochs. However, this approach does not scale well to training with large cohorts, e.g. $`1{,}024`$, which were necessary for experiments with FL with DP.

That is why we implemented another version where every client is trained on 1 GPU and we train the models for several clients in parallel utilizing all available GPUs (e.g. with 32 GPUs we can process $`32`$ clients in parallel). To do that efficiently with highly imbalanced data like CV where some clients have much more data than others, we restrict the training on every client to a pre-defined number of training steps (batches processed) instead of epochs. Switching from a fixed number of epochs to a fixed number of training steps per client was previously reported to improve performance in the presence of data heterogeneity (wang2020tackling).

Since we always use dynamic batching for efficient implementation and the average number of minutes of audio per client in CV is 2.5, FL training with 10 local epochs and total dynamic batch of 2 minutes per client can be approximated with 10 local steps and the same batch size. This configuration is used in all FL with DP experiments.

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/x6.png)

Figure 7: Comparison of WER for FL training between local number of steps (solid) and local number of epochs (dashed). Training is done on CV-en-train with a seed model pre-trained centrally on LS-100. The cohort size is $`S=64`$, total number of central steps is $`T=2`$k, and all other parameters are set the same as in the corresponding configuration in Figure [4](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#S4.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 4.1 Impact of Seed Models and Cohort Size ‣ 4 Empirical Analysis ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping").

Unlike reported by wang2020tackling, we did not observe improved performance after switching to the number of local steps but instead observed degradation in performance: see Figure [7](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A8.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ H.2 Large Cohort Training Implementation ‣ Appendix H Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning with Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping") for the results on one configuration of CV with LS-100 seed model. However, it is of note that the differences will likely get smaller with larger cohort sizes. From results in Figure [7](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A8.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ H.2 Large Cohort Training Implementation ‣ Appendix H Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning with Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"), we expect that more local compute that would be feasible in a real deployment, should lead to better results than what we get in our experiments for FL with DP.

### H.3 Empirical Analysis

For FL training with the large cohort size of $`1{,}024`$, the client delta norms are already bounded due to the local clipping (see Algorithm [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ FL with Differential Privacy (DP) ‣ 2 Federated Learning with Differential Privacy: Background and Notation ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping"), line [1](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#alg1 "Algorithm 1 ‣ FL with Differential Privacy (DP) ‣ 2 Federated Learning with Differential Privacy: Background and Notation ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping")) done in each step of the local training for every client (see Figure [8](https://arxiv.org/html/2310.00098v4#A8.F8 "Figure 8 ‣ H.3 Empirical Analysis ‣ Appendix H Empirical Analysis: Federated Learning with Differential Privacy ‣ Enabling Differentially Private Federated Learning for Speech Recognition: Benchmarks, Adaptive Optimizers and Gradient Clipping")). Local clipping is a necessary part of the training because otherwise the local training of the transformer model would not converge (zhai2023stabilizing; dehghani2023scaling). This is similar to the standard recipe for the central training of transformer models.

![Refer to caption](https://arxiv.org/html/x7.png)

We use exponential decay for central LR starting at $`t=750`$, decay rate $`0.6`$, and transition steps $`750`$ with $`T=2`$k total central steps and $`10`$ local steps. Local (central) LR is 0.2 (0.002).
